= "te ' , The purposes of. this grammar of Aklanon are tos (1) ;
7 _ prowide teachers with ja sourcebook on their dialect, so that they can .
: understaiid the formalities of Aklanon; (2) provide foreign|learners~.
: of the dialect, particularly Peace Corps volunteers or. misgionaries, a
oa with a reference grammar; and (3) provide linguists with a \treataent =~ :
of an unresearched dialect. The grammar is divided into the\following ,
: . “Six ubits: (1) “Preliminary Remarks on Aklanon"; (2) “Phonology. The -
= “ Significant Sounds of the Aklanon Dialect";- (3) “Linguistic a,
: Background’; (4) “Morphophoneaics in Aklanon"; (5) "The Basic Parts .
- +  O£ Speech"; and (6) Function Words. The gramaar is followed by a
. . bibliography of linguistic and dialect study books. (CLK)

Lo . ~ ‘The purposes for this study are tiirce-fold: (1) To provide teachers With a sourcebook- on :
‘ their dialect, so that they can understand the formalities of Aklanon. _With supplementary se-
. ; i. minars and workshops, it is hoped that-more concise and efficient Curriculum guides can be
: : ; prepared for the teaching of the vernacular. Up to the present time there has been much dis-
a agreement abo: : such problems as the spelling of the vernacular, and it is hoped that conven- ee
: tions can be established about the dialect through the agency of this present study. (2) To Te
_ provide fereign learners of the dialect, particularily Peace Corps Volunteers or missionaries,
: . with a reference grammar, covering:the peculiazities of the sound and structure of Aklanon .
: : speech. (3) To provide linguists with a treatment, however basic or humbie, of a hitherto
z ; unresearched dialect; and hence to enrich the catalogue of literature available on the less- .
Poe - known languages or diatects of the world. :
5 _ ” Like any paper or publication, this is an unfinished and\unending work, a part of an on- :
; going and growing process. The particular organization or explanation of the phenomena’ of :
pe the Aklan dialect could very well be debated and changed. No doubt, as time gces on and in- .
a terestin the dialect develops, subsequent works. will be published und may surpass this paper. Y
i This is not, then, to be considered--either by the authors or by the readers~-as a finished / 7
> _ treatise or an absolute statement about the Aklanon dialect; particularily since this edition is an
. the result of much hurried effort to méet a deadline and other requirements. St :
t ‘ This grammar will not be easy to read~-as no grammar is easy to read. It has not beep
an - nade to be difficult, although it has been made to be therough. The reader does Act have to 2.
, come to this book sophisticated in linguistic techniques, but if he keeps with it, he should !eave
. ae with a good deal of sopisticatiba, not only about Aklanon, but about linguistic methods as well.
, ; The study is presented in the light of the above-mtentioned. purposes and qualifications inthe’ —-
/ . ° hope that it may encourage those who absokb it to make their own personal-analysis of the ver~ :
. nacular, for it is only in personal struggle that understanding and knowledge are found. The .
Pe . authors trust, then, thar. it will fill the explicit and projected nccds of the province of Atlan, ,
: ‘the U.S. Peace Corps, and any other similar interested parties.
’. The autbors wish to acknowledge gratefully the help of the following:
wghe Dr. Tommy Anderson of Philippine Normal College for his invaluable linguistic hcip in proof-
_ ! reading and editing this paper, who is greatly responsible for the present format and a good
: . many ideas Contained herein.
; : . The United States Peace Corps for its total support of this project, particularily in the persons .
. of: Dr. Richard Wilson, Mr. John Bossany, Mr. Allan Kulakow, Mrs. Theresa Boucher and
. Mr. Charles Shackelton. The Bureau of Public Schools, Manila inthe person of its directer ,
Juan C. Manuel for theix support.
Mayor Federico O. Icamina and the Municipal Council of Kalibo, who helped in a previous
_ mimeographed edition of this text.
. , : Prof John U. Wolff of Cornell University, Ithaca, New York, whe gave several suggestions ,
. - for various articles in this paper. . .
* Mr. Stephen J. Banta, Peace Corps Volunteer in Kalibo, for proof-reading the entire text and
for suggestions about its format. Mr. Thomas C. Marshall, past Peace Corps in Banga, Aklan,
. for helping in preliminary research of the dialect.
The various citizens of Aklan, for their informant work, given freely and continuaily through-
. out the leng period af this book's composition~-to whom this book is heartily dedicated.

fo, UNIT ONE: PRELIMINARY RBMARKS-ON-AKEANON. — | %
‘ . 1; LOCATION OF AKLANON. Aklanon, which is spelled “Akeanon" by its writers, \
: , . and pronounced /Akganon/” by its speakers, is spoken by some 360 thousand people in
: _ . or bordering on the province of Aklan on the island of Panay in the Philippines. The dia- :
" : lect is somewhat understandable to the people of neighboring provinces and islands who .
so ' speak any one of six West Visayan dialects.“ These dialects are a family of dialects mo,
: . whose ancestor might be called proto-West Visayan, which in turn was a member of the .
i Malayo-Polynesian family of languages, to which such languages as Tagalog and Cebuano :
aa belong. . . — .
é 2. CHART: THE legen: salen FAMILY OF LANGUAGES. Before beginning

r velopment. . : . ao

; ; J, 2. THE AGE DIMENSION. The sécond dimension runs throughout every. individual area
_ ; where Aklanon is spoken. It is a cross-section of all native speakers, and diyides them gen-
Co ~ exally into-three groups: the older speakers (over 55 years old), the middle-aged speakers
“st (over 30 but under 55 years old), and the young speakers (under 29 years old). The older
speech is characterized by so-called "deeper words” (words that are considered archaic), .

ot . The middle-aged speech is considered "standard" and is generally used in exchange conver

(which might be termed "slang"), and short sentence structures. . ' ‘
- 7,3. THE PRESTIGE DIMENSION. ‘The third dimension also rusis throughout the Aklanon-
speaking community and involves what is thought to be a "pure" or "classic" form of the .
dialect. Itis used by religious in sermons and by politicians in campaigns. Anyone attempting ,
to speak in the vernacular at a formal occasion is socially.obliged to speak in this form cr may ‘’
be subject to xidicule, ‘since English is the most commonly accepted (and expected) form of .
- formal communication, Prestige Aklanon is characterized by: . . .
, (1) use of what is thought to bea “classic" or pure form of the dialect. .
‘ For example, of the three. possible words for “province"=-"prohizsya",
° . “lalawigan" (borrowed from Tagalog), and “eacawigan™ (reconstructed _ .
according to general Aklanon derivational.rules)--the word “eaeawigan"
i oo is considered "classic". due to the presence of the fricative /g/-sound,
; ; Generally, where two words exist, and one of them has the /g/, the "_ 7s.
/¢/“word would be preferred, since it is considered""deep" or “pure." =
(2) uge of.longer, more involved or complicated structures. .
(3) in cases where a choice exists between /d/ and /r/, as in the markers
; "ro" and "do", tle /r/*form is preferred, the /d/-form being dismissed -- ‘
oe . as baby-~talk or “inapa" (/‘inapa’/). /d/-forms are generally considered -
” colloquial or careless in formal speech or conversation. ,
° (4) the same intonation and speed used by the older speakers of the dialéc:.
_ 7 it has been the general intention of the authors to record in this text the grammar and
structure of current Aklanon as spoken in or around tke town of Kalibo by members: of the
_ middle-aged community, who represent the most acceptable and idiomatic forms of stan~
7 dard Aklanon. Other differences or admixtures have generally not been taken into, consid~
eration, except in certain instances where close parallels occur, and where such differences ;
are noteworthy.
8. SUMMARY. Aklanon is the dialect of some 360,000 people in ‘ox near the. province
; of Aklan on the northwestern portion of Panay. It is also spoken by Aklan settlers ‘on Palawan ~
. and Mindanao~«especially in Bukidnon and Surigao.” It is characterized by a fricative /s/ :
sound, but otherwise shares many similarities to.the other five West Visayan dialects. The
dialect exists in several forms or dimensions, however the Aklanon spoken in or ayound the
town of Kalibo by the mid “le-aged population is generally under analysis in this present work.

- UNIT TWO: PHONOLOGY, THE SIGNIFICANT SOUNDS OF THE AKLANON DIALECT® i .
; ’ ; 1. INTRODUC.ORY REMARKS. It is the intention of this paper to present the various -"
' . sounds of Aklanon in as simplea manner a8 possible. Keeping in, mind the general nature of
¢ the audience, no attempt has been made to go deeply into the science of phonetics. In most
. . cases, where they are identical or very proximate, the sounds of English #e used as models ;
: : for the sounds of Aklanon. This is done since most of those who may be using this book have ;
: a good knowledge of the sounds and structure of Americana English. In those cases where the
: , sounds significantly differ {rom those of English, certain general linguistic descriptions have :
a become necessary, and they are employed. . .
: . It would be best to advise the reader at the yery beginning that two sorts‘of writing are an
° used in this book: the LOCAL SPELLING (the way the native speakers of Aklanon generally
: . write down their dialect) and 2 PHONEMIC TRANSCRIPTION (a way of writing down the act~ .
P ual sounds uttered by the native speakers, which has been devised by linguists). Generally, ' -
. . ‘the local spelling will be used in this paper. However, wherever such spelling would be in- ;
. . complete far the purposes of linguistic analysis, transcription of the actual.sounds is used. . :
: ; _ This and the following two units make use of such transcription. Spelling is marked by‘quo- =
: . tation marks (as in "bakae" for “buy” in English), while transcription is marked by slanting ~
7 ~ lines (as in /bakag/ for “buy” in English). :
” ; ‘The most common and essential types of sounds are described (a) by being voiced or
yy - voiceless, and (b) by their points of articulation (based on where they are pronounced in ;
4 the mouth): :
. voiced implies receiving sound vibrations from the larynx or voice box. _
: . voiceless implies pot receiving auy sound from the voice box, but merely
ee containing the seund from its own point of articulation. /
- . bilabial means the sounds articulated at the upper Lip by the lower lip.
; dental means the sounds articulated at the tecth. .
, alycolar means the sounds articulated at the alveolar ridge (the guim' behind ; ,
the upper teeth) by the tongue. se,
. velar means the sounds articulated towards the Lack of the mouth by the . .
7 velum or uvula (appendage at the end of the velum). . ,
ve glottal means the sounds articulated deeper in the throat, usually by the i"
. open or closed position of the glottis. , ° \
For those who would like to delve more deeply, further research into more formal texts is
recommended, .
‘ ; 2. STOPS. The characteristic feature of a stop is a complete cutting-off of the outgoing .
air stream. : \
2,1. /p THB VOICELESS BILABIAL STOP [spelled "po" by Aklanons] is formed by closing
° the lips tightly and is never aspirated (spoken with a puff of breath) as in English initial /p/. - .
. Hence, the Aklanon /p/-sound is like the American English /p/ as found in such words as:
oa ; “taps stop, clap, : speed, spend, ; spoon, span" ‘

‘ :2,3. /t/ THE VOICELESS DENTAL STOP {spelled "t" by Aklanons) is usually formed by ..
. ae « .
. . bringing the tip of the tongue against the back of the uppex teeth. In any position in a word
it is unaspirated, unlike the English /t/, which is usually aspirated at the beginning of a ;
‘ word, It sounds somewhat like the American Englist: /t/ in the words: a
; “sit , pit, flat, outdo, _— stand, step" ,
. with the exception that the English /t/ is alveolar (pronounced at the alveolar ridge behind ;
. mo, the teeth) while the Akianon /t/ is dental. x
. 2,4. /d/ THE VOICED DENTAL STOP (spelled "d" by Aklanons]} is formed like /t/, but
with the addition of voice, as in the English words: ,
. “do, dead, bed, said, bedbug, blecding” . :
. with the exception that the English /d/ is alveolar (like the /t/) and the Aklanon is dental. .

S ‘ ° : 2,7. {f/f THE GLOTTAL STOP {usually only spelled in medial position as “-" by Aklanons]} *
* . is produced by an abrupt cutting-off of air by the glottis, or vocal bands. Although it is actu~ er
: rv 4 “ ally quilte frequent in English, it goes unnoticed since it is not essential to meaning and since ~
. . , ~ no real minimal pairs exist. In English, it often appears at the beginning of words which are
: . _. 7.. Spelled as beginning with a vowel; particularily when one is giving contrastive stress as in
: ~~ "2 “Lsaidtam', not tis'\" It also occurs between the two parts of the negative expression often
ye ‘ ca written as "hnh-hoh" which we would transcribe as /a"ah/. .
a , ‘ The glottal stop is generally not spelled out in the common writing of Aklanon. It never °
P is spelled out when it occurs in initial position of words; and no common spelling has been -
ue adopted to represent it in final position of words, though some have tried to introduce the
por Tagalog system of accentuation, the grave /*/ used to show final glottal stop, while the acute :
238 _ . /*/ is used to show stress. The word pronounced /bata/ (meaning “bathrobe") would be .
Se . spelled "bata", while the word pronounced /bata*/ (meaning “young™) would aiso be spelled -
fo . "bata", or else."bat2'"-by those-few who try to note the difference in pronounciation. Since. :
As . the general practice, however, is to leave the final glottal unmarked and to understand which ;
Le - oo ~ word is meant through context, we must accept it as such, since history has proven that com- 3
foo 7 * mon or general practices enduré. This form of spelling obviously presents the foreign learner *
a . ~ with some difficulties, but hé will have to learn the differences just as the Aklanon child does, © °
-* ; . through practice and discovery by context. a
. . Since our present unit is concerned with the phonology aud structure of spoken Aklanon
: . , it is linguistically essential to adopt some symbol which cai be universally used for the ;
glottal stop in all positions: Our choice has been the f'/ since it will remind.the foreign °
7 learner. of its presence, and yet will not interrupt the native speaker's reading or under~ *
. standing of the examples. Some authors (like Reid and Wolff) use the symbol /q/, but use of ..
too this symbol bas proven confusing to both foreign learners and native speakers alike. We jus~ °
o _ ‘ tify our use of /*/ because: (a) it adequately represents the glottal stop and yet is not cumber-
some to the reader; (b) it causes both native speakers and non-native learners of the dialect
, - to pause ang evaluate its meaning, since, after all, it is a true consonant and is so crucial to
many words,deserves transcription and treatment;-and (c) it can be quickly learned and under-
- . stood and ‘should not long interfere with the reading of the examples. We hope this transcrip-
: . tion will be acceptable to all users of this text. *

e uo 2,7. NOTE: There are actually three sorts of final vowel sounds in Aklanon, those that 7
: are closed with the glottal sound, those that end in final /h/~sound, and those that are open. :
The criterion for making this distinction is drawn from the phenomenon of suffixation. When
\ final suffixes are added, for example /-an/ or /-on/, each type of vowel behaves in its own
way. ; . 5;
, (1) THE GLOTTALIZED VOWEL SOUND usually shows consonant gemination (see Unit
Four, Section 9)-~ /bata'/, for example, when suffixed becomes /kabata"*an/, or /putt" / :
becomes /kaputi"an/. -
(2) THE VOWEL FOLLOWED BY /h/ also shows consonant gemination~-/botoh/ generally
; _ ig pronounced as /botohhan/ or /basah/ beconies /basabhon/. . ,
. . . .
. (3) THE OPEN VOWEL is usually lost (if an /a/) or changed to a semivowel (if /i/ or /of}~~
. /pangasawa/ becomes /pangasaw’on/, /"iti/ becomes /ityan/, and /tubo/ becomes /katubwan/
3. FRICATIVES. The characteristic of a fricative is noise produced by the stream of
breath passing through the constricted opening between an articulator and a point of articula~ ;
* , tion. When fricatives are voiced, two kinds of sound are present at the same time: the voice
or glottal tone, and the local friction~noise. ; . .
3,1. /f/ THE VOICELESS LABIO-DENTAL FRICATIVE [spelled as "§" by Aklanons, though
; sometimes reduced, and, hence, spelled as "p"} is produced by bringing the lower lip close to
or against the edge of the upper teeth, so that the breath passing through may be heard. The
sound is not native to Aklanon, but has been adopted occasionally in initial position only in such
\ ’ words as: .
™ . /frangkah/ ("be frank") /farenhiyet/ ("Fahrenheit")
, /filipinas/ ("the Philippines") /filips/ ("Fhilips") 1

This brings us to the end of our discussion of the pure consonant sounds. !n the fol-
. lowing sections, we shall discuss the vowels and the semivowels, which are significantly
“3 different phonemes from the consonants. Vowels are the forms which allow consonants to
é ‘be truly perceived and heard, A consonant, standing alone, is an extremely difficult sound
to hear.

9, VQWELS. have two basic characteristics which distinguish them from any other sounds. © .
eo . & First of al\, they are formed without any Stoppage of the oral cavity or any constriction § .
7. " narrow as to create local sound. Secondly, they are syllabic or nuclear in that they ate the
. . . most prominent sounds in the syllables to which they belong, when taken individually. { In
_ Aklanon, no syllable exists without a vowel, and with the exception of diphthongs (see Stectior
. 10), there is only one vowel per syllable. Just about the only exception to this is the hiss so .
‘e : ; ‘ commonly used to catch the attention of another person, which is syHabic, but which has no -
2 : . vowel at all: /pssst/. . ;
aa ; _ Aklanon bas a total of five vowel sounds, three of which are native to the dialect, and two ,
i a of which have been adopted under Spanish and/or English influence, but which have become :
ie ° phonemic to at least some degree. Before treating each vowel individually, it is best to view . *
a the criteria or dimensions of vowel sounds: 3 ;
: © (a) _ HIGH, MID, and LOW refer to the tongue height or the height of the'highest part of -
be . ~ the tongue during the pronunciation of a Vowel. ‘In the English words
Lo oo “beet, bit, bait, = bet,_~—s bat, ~—ibot™
: - we see the tongue dropping successively (along with the jaw). These six sounds can be sep~
. arated two-by-two into the qualities of: HIGH' ("beet ~ bit"), MID ("bait - bet"), and LOW
; ("bat = bot"). . _
" . (b) FRONT, CENTRAL, and BACK refer to the part of the tongue which is bighest during .
; _ ~ the pronunciation of the vowel, whether it is towards the front, middle or back of the mouth.
: FRONT ("beet"), CENTRAL ("bet"), and BACK ("boot"). -
ne (c) TENSE and LAX refer to the degree of relaxation of the tongue in uttering a vowel.
. a . Generally open syllables have tense vowels, while closed syllables have lax vowels. For
example, in the word /si'in/ (‘where") in Aklanon, with the syllabification being /si--"in/
we find the first /i/ open in its syllable (with no consonant following it and attached to it),
, but the second /i/ is closed (preceded by the glottal /"/ and followed by the /n/). Hence, the ~ -
: , first /i47/si-~/, is TENSE, while the second /i/, /~*in/ is LAX. Since ao vowels differ _
solely with regard to tension, it is not necessary to specify this in the definition of each vowel
sound. That is to say, any vowel can be either tense or lax, depending on its environment.
, (d) ROUND and UNROUND refer to the lips--whether they are relaxed or spread. How-
ever, all Aklanon back vowels are round, and all front and central vowels are unround, and
3 ~no further specification-is fecessary. :
(6 TONG and SHORT refer to the length of pronunciation, which depends very much on
> the individual environment of each vowel. In general, the tendency is that stressed vowels
. are longer than unstressed, and that open vowels are longer than closed. For example, the
. : word /sagamat/ or "saeamat" ("thank you") in Aklanon has three /a/-sounds. The syllabifi-
cation of the word would be /sa~~g4--mat/ with the stress on the penultimate syllable. There
. “are two open vowels (sa--) and (ga~-} and one closed (mat). Of the three, /da/'s vowel is
so : the longest because 1t 1s both open and stressed. Of the remaining two, /sa/'s vowel is
second longest, because 1t 18 open, though not stressed; and /mat/'s vowel is the shortest .

_ 9,2. /e/ MID FRONT VOWEL [spelled "e" by Axlanons] is similar to the soundin the ~
. English words "say" and “they Qf and when it 1s tense in Aklanon); and similar to the sound
. of English "said" and "dead" (if and when it 1s lax in Aklanon). The sound is generally
somewhat diphthongular when it is tense. .
The sound was not native to Aklanon and was borrowed, but since has become phonemic.
. At least one minimal pair exists to show its differentiation from the /i/-sound:
: /pare’/ ("“compadre, gocbrother") /paxi'/ ("priest")

"1 : 10. DIPHTHONGS. A simple description of a diphthong is that it is the flow of one vowel
, ; into another vowel. The technical matter of articulation could be described at great length.
. However, modern or current Aklanon has lost the distinction between a true diphthong and
the simple combination of a vowel and a semivowel, Generally the Aklanon diphthong as it
. - , occurs today can be described as a vowel followed by a semivowel. Instead of calling such
a combination a diphthong, we wall call it-«for our present purposes~-a reduction:

13. STRESS. The preceding consonant and vowel sounds are phonemes or sounds, which,
when put together, make up words which have meauing (morphemes). When these woras are
used in sentences, they will automatically have certain stress patterns, which do not enter
into the direct meaning of tie word. But stress can also be a phoneme, or significant and
meaningful sound, in the Aklanon dialect, since it strongly, influences mcaning. Such a type
of phoneme 1s called a suprasegmental phoneme by Linguists because it secms to be an extra
. layer of structure imposed over the basic segmental phonemes (consonants and vowel@ which
also carry their own sort of meaning. ‘The operation of stress in Aklanon can be seen in two ;
distinct ways. :

7 OT, , ferent meaning. Lock at the following minimal pairs:
: MINIMAL PAIRS ILLMSTRATING STRESS DIFFERENCES & UNRELATED MEANING -

~ 14, PITCH. Pitch is another sort of suprasegmental phoneme. Unlike the phoneme of ,
/ stress, however, pitch doesn't fit into a pattern of individual words, but rather over the
; / pattern of a full sentence-~or sometimes over the pattern of a single word (if it happens to
: / already be a full sentence). Ina language like Chinese, pitch can change the meaning ot @
word. In Aklancn it can only change the meaning or intent of a sentence. Hence, pitch does
not enter into the direct meaning of a word; it only carries its own sort of meaning inio a
sentence pattern. ,
: A full study of the patterns of pitch or intonation throughout the province has yet to be
Undertaken. One of the principal difficulties in such a study is the gi eat degree of variation
; from une area to another of the Aklanon-speaking community. Each town, sometimes even .
. each barrio, has ‘ts own type of intonation or sentence pitch. ,
This present study will therefore do no more than to indicate three types of intonation by
means of commonly accepted symbols, namely /./ for a statement Pitch, /?/ for: a question
pitch, and /*/ for an exclamatory type of intonation. A pause will be indicatety /,/. To
ra {llustrate these symbols we might take a look at the word /iya/ (meaning “here") and the word
/imaw/ (meaning "he" or "she"), Several sentences could be constructed with several possible
intonation patterns, each with a different sort of meaning:
/lya imaw./ ("He is here.")
. /iya imaw?/ ("Is he here?")
~ - /iya imaw’ / ("He is here!)
/itya?: imaw?/ ("Here? He, here?”) and so on.

16. SOME PROBLEMS WITH REGARD TO AKLANON SPELLING. Three problems con-
font the learner of the dialect with regard to spelling. They deserve mention for the benefit
. of those teaching the Vernacular to others, though they are not necessarily problems to native
speakers or spellers.
. 16,1. THE GLOTTAL STOP mught be considered a Potential problem, though it needn't be so, .
since native speakers have generally derived a consistent system: for recognizing it in spelling.
INITIALLY, before vowels, the glottal stcf: 1s understood and need not be spelied out: /amoh/
- (“lord, master") is only spelled "amo". MEMIALLY BETWEEN VOWELS the glottal is undcr-
: stood, since no diphthongs exist in current Aklanon spelling. Thus /ta'6/ (“give; don't know")
will be spelled "tao" and /hu'o/ ("yes") will be spelled "huo". MEDIALLY BETWEEN A VOWEL
AND A CONSONANT, OR WHEN A DOUBLE GLOTTAL APPEARS the glottal is spelled out a5 2
dash ("+"): /man'o/ (“how much?") 1s spelled "man-o". /ba’'oh/ ("turtle") is spelled “ba-o".
/tam'is/ ("sweet"), “tam-is", and /sin'o/ (“who?") is spelled "sin-o". IN FINAL POSITION
the glottal must be learned and/or known by context. It is generally not spelled out, but one
can easily te}] which word is Incant by filling in for sense. Therefore/baho/ ("bass [of sound]")
and /baho'/ ("odor") are both spelled "“baho", yet in a sentcnce one could easily tell which of
the two words was meant.
, 16,2. THE CONSONANT “e" VERSUS THE VOWEL "te" van easily be distinguished. If the
fricative /g/, spelled “e" appears in an env,;ronment with a vowel it will automatically be known

foo . 16,3. THE “o"-"u" SPELLING PROBLEM.” We hve already discussed the pronunciational— os
2 “ . problem of having two distinct phonemes /v/ and:/o/ \ntroduced into Aklanon. Although many .
: : have attempted to devise a foolproof system of spelling, the system augzested by Manuel’ |
¢ Lascrna in 1916 (in his book SUNGKA~AN) has become the most conventional. The following .
: . is a slight revision of his suggestions: « ; a
as . (1) The “u"="o" alternations apply to root words or word bases only. All affixes have - :
4 ” a particular fixed spelling and should not change, no matter \what the sequence of /o/~sounds
zt, ; might be within t! - word. Note the following spellings: . . ;
: “manogitlog" (prefix "manog-" with base "itlog") ;
. “inoghinuesoe" (prefix "inog~" with base “hinuesoe") : .
. -— "tagipuso-on" (suffix "~on" with base "puso" plus prefix "tagi-")
. “sumueunod" (pase "sunod" with "-uin-" imix and /-V,~/ reduplication, "eeu")
. , "ymadto" (base “adto" with "-ume" infix)
~ (2) One syllable words are usually spelled with "o" as in: “ro, do, ko, mo, eon, ron". .
. ‘ . However, one exception exists as a convention. The associste marker is spelled "ku" to dise
tinguish {t from the pronoun form "ko", as in: ’ -
‘ "Ro isda’ hay ginbakac ko." ("The fish was bought by me.") *
"Ro isda* hay ginbakae ku unga’." ("The fish was bought by the child.")
ae ",, baedy ko..." (my house")
a "|. .bavdy ku amo..." ("house of the master")
(3) Inthe case of root words which are identical reduplications (see Unit IV, 10, J) each
, . syllable should be spelled identically according to the way it sounds:
* "Molo" ("grandfather") “bukbuk" (“house gnat")
‘ “conton" ("lower") “kupkup" ("embrace, hug")
“usus" ("slip down") “suksuk" (“house lizard")
(4) In root words of two sylavles, an /o/-sound occuring in the first syllable should be
- spelled "u", and an /o/-sound occuring in the second syllable should be spelled "o". This
‘ applies to ali natuve words, whether or not they have two /o/-sounds in them:
“unga’ " ("child") "suedda" ("enter") . ;
, . "nedn" ("rain") “buedng" — ("medicine") .
"humdy" — ("cooked rice") "eupot" (“paper bag")
“baton” ("receive"’ - “huo" ("yes")
"gaerom" ("{rain} clouu") "hut" ("psyche, interior self")

‘ , 1, NEED FOR RIGOR. Ina more‘or less scientific study of a language or dialect, it
4 . is esseitial to have a more specific concept about the sound and structure of speech and a “
Is language than that common to the "man-on-theSstreet". Vague ideas such as “sound” or; .
: . _ "word" are certainly not enough to grasp the vast treasurehouse of speech, since the .
‘ : rigor of scientific investigation demands a specific name for each unique species or class. - me
; 2. PHONES. On the other hand, scientific linguistics need not be so difficult. Our very
; first stage of study hrings us to the smallest and most basic level--to the separate sounds’ or ’
: phones. We can use an analogy to illustrate the methodology of phonetic analysis. Imagine
° a long file of soldiers marching across a field; all are different individuals, but they can
° be divided into smaller groups for the march. We might divide them into three groups on
. sO the basis of height--tall, medium, and short. Or we could group them into thin, muscular 1
t and fat. Or the grouping could be a combination, such as (a) short and fat, (b) tall and ‘
‘ . thin, (c) talland muscular, and soon. In grouping, we pay attention to similarities and i
, “tend to disregard differences. During this process we might notice a number of civilians 7
: . standing nearby, but we are only interested in the soldiers, so we exclude the civilians from |.
: our grouping process. The same is true of our study of sounds. Just as we cannot accept all \
people for our grouping, for civilians are excluded, so we cannot accept all sound«types or . ;
phones that are found within the sphere of Aklan life and speech. There are sneezes, coughs,
hisses, grunts and other noises which are not part of the language. These are the noises or
. sounds which we must disregard. However, we do take into consideration those particular
sounds or noises which are significant and meaningful.
. 3. PHONEMES. Even if no two people or sounds might be identical] to a point, there are
certainly similarities, and they must complement the group. Ina division of the short and
fat soldiers (mentioned in our ahalogy earlier) as opposed to the tall and thin soldiers, what ‘
would we do with a tall but fat soldier? He does not complement either of the two groups .
that we already have. Even if he were the only one, scientifically speaking we must isolate
him in a special group for tall and fat soldiers. Sucha division of sounds gives us what the .
linguists call a phoneme: a group of one or more phone-types (sounds) which sound the same
(are phonetically similar) and are in complementary distribution. ,
; 4. ALLOPHONES. We have seen, for example, that the /k/~sound exists in Akianon,
It ig usually unaspirated, that is, it is spoken without the extra breath that Ameriéans put on
. the initial /k/. Furthermore, the /k/ occurs in all positions of words or syllables as the ’
7 voiceless velar stop. Hence, /k/ is a phoneme of Aklanon. Whether it occurs in the word °
/kuku/ ("nail {of finger or toe"), /gaki/ ("male, man") ox /pisik/ ("splatter"), it is still
the same /k/. We have seen how some Aklanons pronounce the /k/ as an [x] (voiceless velar
fricative) if it occurs before a /¢/-sound. Instead of saying [akgan]) or (mukgat], they say .
{axgan] or [muxgat}. In Aklanon, this does not change tne meaning any more than a change
in pitch or intonation would change the meaning. Hence, [k] and [x] belong to the same class3
they are said to be allophones of the /k/~sound. Furthermore, if an ordinary American were
to pronounce the word /kamo/ he would most proba ly aspirate his (k). Such an aspirated /k/
might be transcribed as tk}. This sound does occur occasionally in Aklanon, such as when a :
person wishes to emphasize the pronunciation of a /k/, particularily if he might be whispering.
Then we find that tx} belongs to the same class that (kJ and {x} belong to. They are all allo~
phones of one basic sound or phoneme in Aklanon: /k/.

: . 5. FROM'SOUNDS TO WORDS. So far so good. We have seen how the common idea of
. “ *“gound" is not sufficient to carry the load when at least three exact concepts (PHONE, PHO-
NEME, ALLOPHONE) are present. But simple “sounds" when uttered together or in groups .
do not automatically have meaning. When we wish to speak of meaning, we jump from the -
broad class of "sounds" to the class of “words. But here again we must find a more scien~
, . tific approach.
6. MORPHS. A combination of phones or sounds which has meaning is called a morph.
. . But just like phones and the column of soldiers passing by, morphs really happen only once '
y in speech, and then pass on. c
. . 7. ALLOMORPHS AND MORPHEMES. However, like phones, morphs may have simi~
larities of sound, and, in addition, similarities of meaning; if so, then they can be called
allomorphs--a family of meaningful sounds which are the same in both sound and meaning.
For example, in Aklanon we have the word "ikaw" which means "you" in English. Yet .
, "ikaw" can refer to you, the reader, if 1 am speaking; but if you are speaking, then "ikaw"
refers to me, the writer. We know that the sounds of each “ikaw” are the same; but what
° . about the meaning? Would "ikaw" in one sentence be an allomorph of "ikaw" in another?
If we define "ikaw" from the standpoint that it means “you" in the sense of "a singular per~- .
. Son invoived directly in a conversation, who is not the speaker, but who is referred to by ,
- the speaker", then the meaning of "ikaw" is the sane in both cases mentioned.earlier. The .
, ; "ikaw" of the sentence: "ikaw ro nagabasa" ("You are the reader.) and the "ikaw" of the .
; . , > sentence “ikaw ro nagasueat" are allomorphs of the morpheme XikawX. [Note our symbol
here for morpheme, ’X".] Even if the ‘irst “ikaw” refers to one person, and’the second
: to another, the sound of each is the same, and the general meaning (listener, not speaker)
is also the same. Therefore ve satisfy the qualifications for an allomorph--similarity in °
both souad and meaning. . “.
¥ ~ . In the same way that we found the relationship of PHONES, ALLOPHONES, and PHO}#
‘ . NEMES to each other, we find the relationship of MORPHS, ALLOMORPHS, and MOR-
PHEMES. Just as phones are the mere sounds .utterable, while phonemes are the building
. blocks of all the words and vocabulary in a language, so
. morphemes are the building blocks out of which the meaningful
: utterances of speech are put together. A’ morpheme is a group”
‘ . of allomorphs, cach of which is a combination of phonemes;
. but...in structure of the kind that language shares with many
other natural and man-made phenomena, the whole is more than
. . the sum of all ite parts. When phonemes are organized into an
allomorph, meaning is added to make 2 new thing, Just as when .
hydrogen and oxygen are organized into water, a substance emer~
ges that has new and different qualities from its Components... .
Therefore, morphemes, the smallest structural units possessing
" meaning, occupy a key Position in linguistic structure. They are : .
the fugdamental ’ ailding blocks out of which everything we say is .
built. :

: : 8. EXAMPLES OF MORPHEMES. The English word "pages", if thought of broadly, is.

: plural form suffix). The word "meaningful" also strikes:a native speaker as only one simple -
; word, But the word actually has three morphemes: "mean", "-ing” and “-ful". The same
: . soxt of process happens in Ak!anon, “Kahueogan™ (“meaning™) bas three morpbemes or three ..
: ‘ ; separate, significant building blocks: the prefix "ka-", the root word “hueog”™ and the suffix
is . “-an", Even a one-syllable word can be divided into many morphemes, So long as in ic \ -
py there are separate and distinct units of me«ning. Take the word "ring" in English; it is ,
ae & one morpheme-+a root word with the meaning “to make 2 bell sound”. But the same series a
1 , . of phones making up the word "ring" in Aklanon is very different. ta Aklanon, the word :
io: "ring" is composed of three morphemes: "r-" (which is the shortened form of "ro" [the to- :
7 Loot pic marker}), "-i-" (which is the abbreviation for "imo", the pronoun for "your™), and “-ng" :
: (which is the shortened form of "nga" which is the linking marker). Hence, "ring", the ab- ‘ .
: . breviation for "ro imo nga" may be only one syllable or ove word in Aklgnon, yet st bas three
a FY morphemes, because it contain three separate meanings. One can see how the old classic
“ terms of "sound" and "word" are misleading, and why the linguist must apply more pxecision
' . to his science. . NO ~
. “~ _ Just as we discovered that the Phones [k], (x] and Ky in Aklanon are all allophones or e
. parts of the same phoneme /k/, so we will find that certain morphs are allomorphs of the
same morpiieme. . an .
. . For example, we know in English that the plural is usually marked by the suffix spelled
- . _. “es"' or "*es". However,-in reality the pronunciation is threefold, namely /-s/, /~z/ or
/+z/, a8 in the three respective plurals for "ant", “floor", and “pridge". We have, there- .
fore, three allomorphs for the plural morpheme in English. ;

9. HOMOPHONES. Sometimes we find two identical sounds, but with two very different
. . meanings. For ‘example, in Aklanon we have the combination /tab6’/ which can mean “hap- ’
pen" or "a dipper or ladle". Phonologically they are identical, and are called homophones ‘
Sp {words which sound the same); but semantically they are different (since they have different
. meanings). Sometimes these homophones can cause a considerable degree of confusion, so
i. at is good to keep them in mind.
A classic case of homophones in Aklanon occurs with the combination /ka/. Look at the
. following sentences:

UNIT FOUR: MORPHOPHONEMICS IN AKLANON : .
Aklanon has two sorts of root words. There are the normal or simple roots, which repre-
° sent the root word 1n its full form. There are also the reduced or changed rcevuts, which repre-
sent the root word after some sort of morphophonemic change has occured. , -~
* For example, we have the normal root "bakae™ /bakag/ ("buy"). When this root is suffixed
with -an Or’ -on, this root changes, the full forms being "bakean" /bakgan/ and "bakeon"
/dakgon/. In this case, the reduced root of "bakae" becomes "bake-" /bakg-/. What has hap-
. pened here? We have lost the vowel /a/ and the word 1s also resyllabified. Normally we find
the sylNable division /ba--kag/, but with the reduced root the sylabification is /bak--¢-/.
This is one form of morphophonemic change. Altogether in Aklanon, there are ten such types
of change. . ;
. 1. STRESS CHANGE occurs when the simple root word is expanded by affixation to other
. members of the word range, particularily with suffixation. But it also occurs on occasion, as ‘
in English, on a word which changes function from a verb to a noun (e.g. the English noun
. “présent" and the verb "presént'). 7

8. METATHESIS 15 the process whereby two consonant morphemes change the:r position. ° |
Itis not a simple operation, linguistically speaking, since three changes take place: (a) loss |
of the syllable's vowel sound; (b) metathesis or switching around of the consonants; and (c) .
movement of the new initial consonant phoneme to the final position of the preceding syllable
(refer to this unit, Section 2 and 3). Let us follow this operation step by step with the word |
. finéra/ ("drink"). First (a) the final vowel is lost, leaving us with the root /inms; second |
; (b) the two consonants metathesize or switch around, giving us the form /imn/; then (c) the |
syllable division results as /‘ini--na/ (with the suffix "-a" being added), from the initial di-
vision /‘i--nont/.

that these words were borrowed as one syllable words from Chinese or Indian languages, but

redupincaced as time went on. Manuc! went or to prove that many of the one-syllable versions
of these worus did, in effect, correspond with Chinese werds with similar meanings. Whether
these woids were actually borrowed from Chinese, or whether both the Chinese words and the
f~ Philippine versions stem frum a Consmon stream 18 not our question here. We only mention the
- theory as one account for the oc. urance of such words in Aklanon. Such words do not present
any morphophoneini¢ problem today either, since the Changes that occured took place far in the
Past. However, they are mentioned at this ime to show the nature of the reduplication within

_ 10,6. REDUPLICATION OF NASAL AND FIRST VOWEL AFTER /mang-/. The prefix /mang/

of "mang-" (which here is /n/ after assimilation) is reduplicated along with the first vowel of

us the form "manunudlo’" [reduplicated form is underlined] meaning "teacher", v

. A. WHAT IS GRAMMAR? -
. ‘Thus far we have been discussing individual units: units of sound and larger units of sound SO
combinations and meaning, words. ,
. When we put words together, they are not just thrown into speech haphazardly. Specific or-
der, or structure, is called for; and without such structure meaning cannot be coaveyed fram
speaker to hearer. This building of words into larger organized combinations or structyres ts
what is meant by grammar. Grammar is @ level beyond the lexical meaning of each individual = -
word. For example, we ali know the meaning of the words “hall, town and beautiful". Yet, if
; we put them in that order, we would use the idiom “meaningless” to describe such an arrange-
- ment: “hall town beautiful”. Now we say that this arrangement is meaningless, ndt because the
individual elements are meaningless, but rather because the combination of those three mean- .
ingful elements ig not understandable--at least when given in that particular order. Thus, or-
— der or structure is is::portant in the giving of meaning. c-
. On the other band, if 1 say “beautiful town hall", I have combined three meaningful words :
; or elements into a larger structure which is also meaningful. Grammar is a level of meaning
~. beyond the literal meaning of each individual element in a statement. .
From the point. of view of the Listener, there are five kinds of signals which reveal the
patterns of structural meaning in which words ay@arranged. We shall briefly discuss them,
one by one. ,
4. WORD ORDER is the linear or time sequence in which words appear in an utterance. .
‘ Without correct word order, statements are difficult if not impossible to understand. Word .
order is a key point in the conveyance of meaning. Certain levels of word order occur’ those
most commonly used and accepted by native speakers of a language, those which can be under-
re stood with difficulty and sound "foreign" to a native speaker, and those which are unintelligible
. to a@ mative speaker. ‘
Every language has certain strict limitations on word order. In English the position of «
, word in @ sentence is important to its meaning. Poor word order often can confuse the meaning.
Look at the following series of words: "sold the fish women the”. This series is certainly not ‘
structured properly according to the canons of English grammar, and is consequently meaning~-
: . legs. It might make a good word assembly game, but in speech which is trying to communicate
particularily on a rapid, day-to-day basis-~it would never be understandable or uaderstoot'.
If we were to rearrange the series this way: “sold the women the fish” { becomes @ bit
more understandable, but it is not naturai word order, and it ts still confusing. One cannot
tell for sure from the grammar who {g doing the seiling, and what: as sold (though sense can
fill this type of information). Someone who tried to understand the above statemeat would do
some mental reconstruction, correcting the word order to "The women sold the fish.” or to
“The fish sold the women." Of course, ‘iis sense would prefer the first correctior, unless he
were listening to some sort of fairy tale waere fis: actually were doing the selling. It*ts ‘only
at this final point that we have a meaningful, grammatically correct statement.
In AkJanon, word order strikes a nun-native speaker [particularily a speaker of English] as
being quite louse or free. For example, the above example of English could be translated:

2. PROSODY. ‘Prosody is the over-all musical pattern of stress, pitch, and juncture in 27
which the words of an utterance are spoken. The various possibilities of intonation have been
‘ mentioned already (sce Unit Il, Section 14).- It neex only be pointed out here that learning to
. . ; express oneself musically within the language is important. 4f one gives a comming form of
intonation or pitch to an intended polite réquest, it no longer means what the speak might .
' have intended; in effect, it will be a command, and the response from the listener will corres- ..
' Pond to the conveyed meaning and not the-intended meaning. Instead of simply getting someone! - ;
to do something politely, the speaker (in this casé) might inspire an emotion of fear, anger, or
. simple misunderstanding. It is not so much, therefore, what is said, but also how it is said .
(or sung) which plays into the prosody of speech. . .
- Mistaken levels of pitch, stress, or intonation either sound foreign, or can--at: tlieir worst ,
an +~confuse or delete meaning. Many word exist where the difference in stress can be the dif~-
ference between an inocuous ward and a so-called "dirty word". Propriety, of course, pre-
, vents us from quoting such examples, but all members of the Aklanon speech community gere
- well aware of such differences. Some differences in meaning have also been shown (see Unit
. Il, Section 13 and Unit IV, Section 1, 1-2) to be involved ~-ith stress. A declarative intonation :
Pattern when a Question pattern is meant can ruin the conemaitye@e discussion. Such differen-
\ ces as these show that prosody is also an important pazt of the-grammar of Aklanon upeech. .

‘ 3. FUNCTION WORDS. There arg two basic classes of words, an open class and a closed ° |
class. The open class of words is geiferally characterized by the varigus parts of speech, while
: the closed class is characterized by funccion words. The open class of words provides a very
wide choice of meanings (referring to things or events in the so-called “real world”, the world .
. of speech) and gives the speaker a great deai of freedom to choose. In the stuucture "The
was born yesterday.", for example, we have a wide variety of choices possible, such as: “cat,
_ dog, talf, baby, boy, girl, lamb" and so os. . ‘ .
‘ In the structure "_____ baby was born yesterday” we do not have a wide variety of choices.

can We must choose from the function class, and our choice is limited by both grammar and sense.

’ . like “one”, "some" or "any". But the idea Is that our choice is limited. i
: _ ‘Function words are a closed class of words and/or morphemes which emhody necessary _°
. distinctions and also embody cases where distinctions are relatively few in number. Their basic
characteristic as a class is that they have a Small or limited range of meaning, and they pre-
sent the speaker with a forced choice; he must simply choose one or the other of them. This is -
to say that the meaning a function word has to convey has to be (and must be) conveyed. The
. very structure of the language forces it on you. , . ot
For example, in Aklanon the structure "“Mayadayad do baeay" is possible, meaning "The ‘
house is beautiful.": The speaker could use any one of many words in the position of "baeay”
("house"). He could, for example, have said "payag" ("hut"), or “eskuylahan” ("school"), or ‘
. “simbaban” ("church"). He could describe the beauty of countless things. But in his descrip- .
‘, . tion, he must use the topic marker "do". It is obligatory-~it's allomorph "ro" could also be
: used; but one or the other of the allomorphs for the topic marker must be chosen. There is no
: freedom involved. The language forces the structure upon you.
' Another example of function words can be seen with the English prepositions. They are ‘
function words, and they force you to commit yourself, Each has its own specific meaning, .
. a as in: °
“by the house” .
: "at the house" ,
‘ ° . "in the house"
. "to the house” ' . : .
. “into the house” = * .
. “with the house”
‘ The speaker of English is forced to make a commitment. In Aklanon, however, sucha set
. . does not exist, and the language permits the speaker to take a vague way out with "sa baeay”
L which could mean any one of the above mentioned English phrases. This is net to say, of
. course, that Aklanon cannot express the above prepositional meanings, for it can; but it does .
so in a different way, not with function words, but with actual parts of speech: ’
"ga kilid it baedy" ("at the side of the house") ~
' ‘ "ga suedd it baedy” ("in[to] the house") ;
"sa idacum it baedy" ("under[neath} the house") \
; Note that we still have the hasic phrase "sa...baeay", but with other qualifiers that maxc the
: meaning more explicit. .
We might envision function words as the more humble servants of the unpressive lexicai

5. DERIVATIONAL CONTRAST is the difference between words which have the same base |
or root, but which differ in the number and nature of their derivational affixes. In other words,
: it is the difference between various forms of affixes which change words from one part of speech ,
co to another. It is a free class cf grammatical units, 2s opposed to inflect‘ons which 2re the func- )
_ tion class of grammatical units. Examples of such are given in the following list: _

Note how the root “tawo" 1s transformed by the various prefixes or suffixes into different parts |
of speech with different syntactica} (grammatical) meanings. Each retains the basic meaning
. of the root word, but contrastive meanings exist between each particular division, or derivation. |
. Derivational contrast differs from inflection in that inflection is concerned with the various |
forms of a root word within a particular part of speech (e.g. the conjugation of a verb or the |
comparison of an adjective form-class), while derivational contrast is concerned with the dif- |
. ferences of both use and meaning of a root within the different parts of speech. For example, }
in the Enghsh "the happily man lazy watched the people go by", we know two mirtakes occur. |

6. SUMMARY. It is the aboye five elements which make up the corpus of grammatical
meaning in Aklanon. When we discuss, then, the “grammar of Aklanon" we are principally
referring to them. The common notion of speaking with "good grammar” or "bad grammar"
is not bound up with this more precise meaning of "grammar", namely the level of meaning
a bad imposed upon individual words when they are grouped together into larger structures to con-
; ° vey meaning. . ,
4 ; If the first four units of this book dealt with letters and words, it did so only to prepare
the reader for the greater combinations that lje ahead. The putting together of words into
sentences, however, is not a ready-made process: neither for the infant nor for the foreign- -
: , learner. Taking a random group of words which a baby might babble or a foreigner might
° ° ‘ mimick is not to imply that we have either a statement or meaning, or that @ Person can .
. speak the dialect. \2 have seen how words with meaning are not the only requirement for a ;
. statement; there must be a meaningful combination of meaningful words--in other words, we
. : need both lexical and structural meaning to build sentences.
Lexical meaning refers generally to the meaning words have which can be found in a
dictionary. It refers to the things that go on about us in the world, not only the real world
_ of "trees" and "rivers" and “people”, but also in the world af "thought", “belief”, "super-
os -  stition", “hope” and "love". It is the type of meaning which we can try to define or explain ws
to another, the very core of our conversations and communications.
wv
Structural meaning, on the other hand, is the way meaning gets put into words, sentences,
- and communication. It is the surt of thing we have been describing in this unit by wore order> ‘
. prosody, function words, inflection and derivational contrast. .
\The remainder of this unit will be dedicated to the four basic parts of speech: the regular
‘ and stative verbs, noun and/or substantives, adjectives and adverbs. It is these units and
thelr function within te sentence which lay the groundwork for the building up of meaningful
. : utterance§ and rational communication, These words constitute the bulk of all words with .
the above-mentioned lex:cal meaning. ‘hey are the most important single step in our ae
: covery of the why and how of the Akianon tongue.

B. THE REGULAR VERB ,
° /
. 1. THEORY. Verbs are the most important part of any utterance , One cannot speak long
in sentences which have no verbs. Verbs are present~-really or implied~-in almost every ut-
terance. Because of the key importance of the verb in Aklanon, not,only due to the frequency
of its occurance and to the fact that it occurs first in a sentence ou many éccasions, but also
because of the way whole sentences are built explicitly about or around the verb form, it is
not surprising why we choose to discuss it first among the basic Parts of speech. :
Despite the importance of the verb, and its frequency of occurance, it is probably the most
elusive part of speech to define.
Verbs are the hardest of ali linguistic concepts to define, because in Linguis-
tic definition the verb is the starting point, the basis on which other definitions
: rest. It is improbable that a definition can be devised which will have meaning
. to anyone who does not already know, more or, less, what a verb is. Beginners ‘
can best be taught what verbs are by having verhs pointed out to them. If a -
oo. child asks uS what a tree is, we do not inform him that it is a perennial plant aa .
supported by a large stem, or trunk, of wood; we show him a tree, or seyerat~~
. trees, and then he knows what a tree is. In a similar wa recognize
. verbs, and when we can recognize them, then ite to undertake the task
of defining and analyzing them. The student who can learn to recognize verbs .
can be taught the rest of grammar. Those who cannot learn to recognize verhs,
however many verbs are Pointed out to them, cannot be taught anything about
. grammar. 1 * _
This extract above itlustrates the importance of verbs, and yet their clusiveness to formal de- ,
finition. This was said with reference to verbs in English; the importance of verbs within
- Philippine languages was similarily stressed by McKaughan:
Before turning to the thesis proper, we would like to emphasize the importance
of describing verbal inflectional categories in the study of Philippine languages.
We had at one time emphasized the analysis of the particles or pronouns as re-
Jation markers <lmost to the exclusion of verbal inflection. It now seems to us
. that the primary key to sentence structure is to be found in the verb where basic
syntactic relations are indicated, and which js the center of most major sentence
. types. Further, we ar’ convinced that categories similar to those we have de-
scribed for Maranao occ also in the other Philippine languages. 12
Such is true, we shall see, for the Aklanon verb system: the verb is a c@nter anda key. :

starting point.

1,5. VERB DETERMINERS. Perhaps the easiest way to find out whether or not a word is a .
verb or another past of ‘speech is through the verb determining function words. Members of
this class are drawn from several parts of speech, some of them are verbs, others are adjec- |
® tives or discourse particles when reduced to their basic root; but all of them can function to |
itlustrate or point out a verb.
, The class of verb determiners is quite large, and some of them allow for more than one .
: verb form after then; but the largest class can be found with the verb prefix mag~. A formal
. diagrar of this construction would be as follows:
Lo, . {verb determiner] ~ [subject] « mag~ [verb root] ~ {remainder of the sentence]
This system is quite infallible for unearthing verb roots. Take a look at the following example:
' "Naila’ ako magadto sa Manila’." .
("I want to go to Manila.")
The verb determiner here is “naila'"; note the verb root “adto" following the mag~ verb prefix.
Because of the presence of the verb determiner and the mag-~ prefix, any verb stem can be il~ .
lustrated. ‘The following 1s a list of the most common verb determiners:

™., 2. TIME is the one thing unique to verbs apart from the other parts of speech. It is also
a ‘ the easiest function of a verb to grasp, and, in either case, is a natural starting point for our
discussion on verbs.
° , In Aklanon, there are different ways of telling time. The Philosophers tell us that the
.. . events of the world about us are entrenched not only in space, but also in time. Nat. orly do
events happen in space and time, but people like Emmanuel Kant and Albert Binstein have
' shown us thet our thoughts are also relative to space and time. Time is one of thuse things
pervading our thinking and our being. When we get down to putting across in speect: the idea
or notion of time, there are two Mechanisms in the Aklanon dialect.
First of all, we can say the time explicitly: . ’

. kabi-i (last night") ku 1962 (“in 1962 [past. time]")
. : hinduna‘ (“later on”) hin-aga (“tomorrow”) °
. makarén ("now") . sa ulihi (later on")

‘ fyture time. Thus, verbs are inflected for time; the forms that are attached to the root words
say something or other about the time of the action--whether it be past, present or future.
2,1. ASPECT. The first genera! notion of time in the verb is given by one of two! basic
morphemes denoting aspect, or the degree of completion of an action. All verbs have forms ~ .
: which show one or the other of aspect values; in ‘addition, at least some of the forms of every
. verb illustrate either the na- morpheme or the ma- morpheme, each of which reveals the as-
- pect of the verb. .
(1) THE REAL ASPECT (na-)!4 denotes that the action of the verb has already begun. It_
sayé no more and no less. We only know that the action is already a reality. Whether it is
going on currently, or recently completed, or finished long ago cannot be known from the form
itself. We only know that the action has begun:
naila’ aké. ("I like" or “I liked")

os ‘9.3. SUMMARY: TIME AND THE AKLANON VERB. We Have seen how explicit time is not”
- given by the verb in Aklaaon, but rather through adverbs or adverbial statements, or else by
. time particles. The verb itself is relegated to aspect, the degree of completion f en action.
There are two basic aspects: the real (generally magked by na-) :nd the unreal (generally markec
. by ma-). The real aspect indicates that the action dxypressed by the verb has already begun, and
it has further distinctions of imperfective, perfectiv> the past conditioned forms. The unrea.
aspect states that the action expressed by the a ot yet begun, and it has further distinc~ ;
tions of expected, fururc, subjunctive, dependent, uture conditioned. There is also a par-
; ticiple forin of the verb which is abstract in general, though it can be used to express past ime
x in dependent clauses. ; °

) 4. ROLE.“ Before we go into the intricate mechanisms of focus involved with the Akla- .
. *non verb, we can simplify matters quite a bit by understanding the concept a! role,
The whole idea of language is the communication of facts about the real world. When things
. happen, we usually want to talk about them. But when things happen they arc relative to a gooa
. many factors. An action, for example, is not merely related to itself in terms of the vocabu~
. “T Jary used to describe it. Kaon or “cat” does not merely happen by itself. The process of eat- ,
‘ ing happens: ‘ - .
- at a certain time: perhaps in the morning (breakfast), at noontime ,
; . (lunch), or in the evening (supper or dinnex); .
- ina certain place: perhaps in the kitchen, or the dining room, or
at a picnic at the beach, in a restaurant or at the lurch counter;
’ , - with a definite actor: perhaps you or I are eating, perhaps Jose or
4 _ Maris, J: maybe that man or child, a carabao, 2 bird, or q kitten; -
. , - wt! J ucfnte object: perhaps rice, meat, vegetables, fruits, or
. . ; fish, dessert, or a snack;
- with some sort of instrument: one eats with one's hands, or else
with a spoon and fork; we also use plates and bowls; — .
. and various other SITUATIONAL DETAILS, expressed or implied, such as a beneficiary,
a, cause or reason for the action, anu co on.
. In addition to these relationships wichin the ‘real world of events, the speaker can introduce
, his own personal bias or emphasis in narrating the action, particularily to show his own point
: cf view, his own personality, or to sway or influence the listener. In other words, he can
~ color the facts for one reason or another. & :

. situational roles. They are all the possibilities or relations or situations around an action

. that tongue must foliow these ways. Language can not and does not allow for total freedom of

sbuut, and who did not know its partucular name. a ;

‘ 5. FOCUS. Verbs, we had seen, are the most important part of speech. In-our syntac~ /
; . tic definition we saw how the verb is a center, and how the other parts of speech in the sen-
tence are made to fit around that center. The mechanism through which this occurs 18 called
"FOCUS". FOCUS IS THE GRAMMATICAL RELATIONSH:P SIGNALLED BY THE VERBAL
AFFIX TO A CERTAIN PHRASE IN THAT CLAUSE OR SENTENCE. If verbs are.the most
. important single parts of a sentence, thén focus is the most important single grammatical
: function in the Aklanon dialect, since (a) it determines the construction of the entire sentence,
and (b) it is the way that the Aklanon language permits its speakers to discuss actions in the
factual world meaningfully. °
. We mentioned that, apart from the various situationa? details or roles, the speaker, when 7
. describing the environment around him, adds an element of bias or emphasis to what he is
saying. In other words, the speaker never really narrates what actually happened~-to do 60
would literally have to recreate the entire scene. Instead, he gives his interpretation of the
- activity. The question of just how close one can come to "the real thing” we will leave to the
. .? , . philosophers; the problem at hand is simply that, when describing the environment, a pergon
- _ can only’say what he saw and how. he saw it. ot
~~ . -
. , 5,1. STRESS IN LANGUAGE. Part of the limitations are not only in “he speaker's way of
. ‘seeing, hearing and perceiving. The mechanism of language itaelf also imposes some bias
or emphasis factor. Fach language handles stress in its own way.
' English, for example, uses stress and pitch, as shown by the underlining in the fallowing
examples: ’ .
‘ - “What! He was the one who did it?"
: "T said“x6 not ‘yes'.” .
a "Do you really believe that?” .
‘ "Do you really believe that?”
"Do you really believe that?” . .
"Do you really believe that?”
"He went home." : .
7 “He went home.” '
5,2. STRESS THROUGH FOCUS IN AKLANON. Aklanon, ont’ e other hand, shows its
emphasis primarily through FOCUS~-an agreement relationship ‘hich consists of a verb
form and related function-marked phrases that reveal the empha is patterns of the speaker.
It is en agreement relationship because the entire construction is bound together in gram~
matica] relationship to itself, independent of any other forms outside the construction, and
the various elements of the construction are tightly tied together.
. The elements of the construction in Aklanon are universally:
41) a verb, with its proper focus affixes;
; (2) a topic phrase, which is focused upon, which receives the
signal from the predicate, and which 1s marked by some
, morpheme [usually ro/do, a topic pronoun, si/sanday, or
a topic deictic] as the topic or key point of the clausc; °
. (3) other related associate, object or referent phrases givi ig
one or more ‘of the situstional details. .

; ; 5,7: FOCUS IN AKLANON IS NOT THE SAME AS VOICE IN ENGLISH Veny often, focus _.
; and voice are considered to be identical or parallel constructions m the two different languages. ™
an ' Yet the differences between focus in Aklanon and voice in English set eaclh apart 4s a distinct
ad feature of its own tongue, and should be noted well. \ ‘
, i *s
> (1) DE~EMPHASIS OF SUBJECT VERSUS STRESS ON OBJECT. The pagsive voice in Enge -
>» | . lish, if stated without the subject, can be used to de~emphasize or deny the existence of the
’ subject. Inthe sentence, 4 ° ;
Sat (aj) "The banana was eaten." \ :
: ° the subject may simply not be important\ ox it may be unknown. The English\takes the sub- :
; a ject and, as it were, “hides it under the,rug." We do not and can not know wh did the action. ‘
; In Aklanon, however, the Object Focus does not de-emphasize the subject, ‘but xather in- a
definitizes it. Moreover, it explicitly emphasizes, ‘points out and stresses the object or goal
; , of the action. For example, \e
: (ao) “Kinaon ro saging." ¢ a
can be considered parallel to anothex Akianon statement-type, namely the-€xistential:
a (a3) “May kumaon it saging." : ' : .
\ ("Somebody ate the banana.") ; oa :
The may (existential) construction, however, nneed pecifically indefinitizes the sub-
ject, while the Object Focus implicitly indefinitizes theSubyect or doer of the action.
4 . s
Thus, with regard to the subject in English passive sentences and Aklanon Object Focus ;
sentences, when either language does not state the subject, English purposely de-emphasizes
the subject, while Aklanon implicitly indefrhitizes it.
, , (2) TREMENDOUS EMPHASIS 10 SUBJECT VERSUS TREMENDOUS EMPHASIS TO OBJECT.
. The passive voice in English, xf stated with the subject, is used to give tremendous emphasis .
to the subject. It drives the actor or doer of the action home with all the force the language
can offer: ‘ “ "
. . (by) "The-banana was eaten by George.
. Here, “George", the doer of the action, is stressed, given special emphasis, singled out. ;
In Aklanon, on the other hand, when the subject is stated in an Object Focus sentence, it
is only given és information. The object or goal of the action is given tremesdous emphasis,
driven hope with all the force the language can offer, but not the actor or doer of tlle action:
7 — |
Lo (by) "Kinaon ro saging ni George."
It still does not matter who ate the banana 1n ths focus, but rather the fact that it vas a banana
_ Which was eaten. In AKlanon, the parallel to stressing che subject of the sentence or doer of
v the action (as 1n English passive voice with a statement of the actor) is found in the Actor Focus:
a ;
vo ~ 83 -
a , ‘
a , )
van \ -
x Ie
(<)
\ . :
=== PDF PAGE 102 ===
my . ‘ o {
. | a ; ~ |
. \ ’ ; . . . ; ‘ ' “4 ‘
(b3). "Si George ro nagkaon it saging." :
(‘George was the one who ate the banana.") * . ;
. [Although the appropriate forceful English idiom should be:
. "The banana was eaten by George.")
Thus, when the subject is stated, the English passive voice stresses it with a great deal ,
of force; while the Aklanon Object Focus still stresses the object (not the actar) of the.action
. with great force, with the doer of the action still remaining indefinite, unstressed, incidental
to the action. .
. (3) Most importantly, English voice only permits TWO TYPES OF CONSTRUCTIONS: .
° the active voice and the passive. Aklanon, on the other hand, has FOUR DIFFERENT FO-
CUSES: Actor, Instrument, Object and Referent Focus classes. Even if some see a paral-
lel between English active voice and Aklanon Actor Focus, and between English passive
Ue woice and Aklanon Object Focus, there is no equivalent parallel in English for Aklanon
Instrument and Referent Focuses. However, as we have pointed out, the parallelism between ;
. voice and focus is a forced one, since each language has its own points of emphasis [see (1)
ot “and (2) in this section}.
. Due to this general lack of parallelism between English and Aklanon verbal statements,
- the translator 1s left with several problems and several possible solutions. In the immedi- _
ately, following section, we will discuss a paradigm in Aklanon. The following are several ‘
. idiomatic and litera] translations that attempt to catch the mood and character of the mech-
anisms underlying focus [and the meaning of focus] in Aklanon. -
Taking the sentence examples used 1n this section 5,5, we,can offer two possible English “
‘ translations. The first method underlines the stressed word, which would denote,some stress .
or inflection made by the voice in Lnghsh, to give emphasis (wnile Aklanon, of course, gives
stress through the topic position and equivalent focus). The second method in English is to ‘
; show stress through a relative phrase, such as: "It is the. ..who/which will..." (for future~
‘ oriented sentences). Please refer to examples (a3) through (d3*) on pages 78-79. .
Me : (a3) ACTOR FOCUS with focus on the actor or doer of the action: *
. "The man will buy bread for the child." .
"It is the man who will buy bread fox the child." “
(b3) INSTRUMENT FOCUS with focus on the instrament of the action:
; , "The man will buy bread for the child with the money." 2
: . "It is the money which the man will fuse to] buy bread for the child." |
{c3) OBJECT FOCUS with focus on the direct object or goa! of the action: ,
. “The man will buy bread for the child with the money." :
“It is the bread which the man will buy for.the child with the money.” 2

: 5,8. A PARADIGM 1s a system of parallel variations within a single linguistic environment.
The name is derived from: the Greek word for “pattern”, since a paradigm is basically a way
of revealing the patterns of a language’s grammar. ‘Paradigms are actually linguistic inven- a
; ’ tions used to illustrate the structurg_of a language; to this degree they are not real represen~
. . tations of speech. _ ° . °
. When a native speaker looks. at a paradigm in his own native language, he is likely to have . ,
: . some reservations in accepting it, particularily since it sounds or looks Strange to him. It
~ does not sound like anything he would normally say. That is because paradigms ¢re basically
sedagogical; they are used for teaching the language to anyone who is studying it. Hence, if
pedagogical; they ed for teaching the language to any ying e
‘ they sound artificial to a native, this in no way should lesson their weight, since, in effect,
- they are artificial. T.wy are invented and used to teach with, and not to speak with. So if
. they do not represent the actual way a person speaks, there is no real problem with that, so
long as they accurately represent the structure of the language he is speaking. ;
, An example of a paradigm in English would be:
7 . "] write a story."
"lam writing a story." ‘ .
= “Il wrote a story."
oo "I was writing a story."
J . “I will write a story." .
‘ "I will be writing a-story." . .
7 . . "I have written a story.” 7
. "| have been writing a story."
and soon. The above paradigm shows changes 1n tense in English; beyond that, 1t shows |
simple as opposed to pr gressive tenses, and they are alligned in pairs. No speech, no con~ |
versation in standard American Enghsh would ever sound like that; yet the paradigm: has value |
because each individual eleaient of it represents a true concept in and eacerpt from good Eng- |
lish speech. The uidividual elements are correct and meamngiul. The entire paradigm Clear-  ” |
ly describes certain structurcs of Enghsi, and 1s also useful in leaching those structures.

5,9. AN ANALOGY EXPLAINING FOCUS. Anyone who knows how to operate a|camera ca
kuows that when the photographer has something to photograph, he focuses on it, and all of
the parts of the camera (leus, body, aperature, shutter speed, and so on) must cdrresond ‘
to the subject matter. For example, if the man points his camera West to take a picture of ;
- the mountains, but the body is facing East, when the shutter is snapped; then the picture wil
; not Se of the mountains, though it may be of the sea, or something else that was in,the view:
of the camera. But the point is, the entire camera must correspond to the wish of the photo-
gtapher. He must aim the camera properly, focus properly on the subject. matter (or else
the picture will be blurred), open the lens and/set the shutter accurately (or else the picture
, ‘ will be too bright or too dark). [* ° .
. . i
Roughly the same is true when speaking Aklanon. Whatever the speaker decided to focus
on is made the topic of the sentence (with ro or a similar topic function marker or function
. word), and the verb form and all other forms in the sentence (be they actor, goal, instrument,
location, or anything else) must correspond. Putting ro on the actor, and then using Object
Focus makes a direct object of the actor, whether one wants to or not. It 1s hke wanting to.
take a picture of a beautiful lady, but aiming at a nearby tree instead. What the photoprapher
wants to portray, and what the speaker wants to say, depend on a lot more than will power or .
~~ desire. Many things must be mampulated first~-be they lens; bellows, shutter speed and ap-
. erature--or topic form, verba! affix, focus, aspect, and the various function markers and
slots. :

a . . o. SOME NOTES ON VERB STEM CLASSIFICATION. Although a complete chart of verb
“ ; - affixes will be presented in the neat section, it must be noted that not every verb root can be
my ~o inflected for all of the focuses. The classification of verb roots in Aklanon is not a simple .
so _ matter. It is one thing, for example, to know that kaon ("eat") is a verb root; but itis quite -
yo, . another thing to know how it is used, what various focus changes mean with that root, ard ;
my, , what particular affixes or affix-corubinations are possible. Such a lengthy study has not yet :
i wo been undertaken. It’has been our purpose in this volume te record the possible forms. Ina
; eG . succeeding volunne (the dictionary), We hope'to inciude each individual verb stem, and to re-
: fe ~ . cord the frequency of its usage W@h various affixes or affix-groups. ‘ ee
yo . Basically, the Problem is one of coordinating what situational roles are possible with the
. ; grammatical role of cach verb. For example, some verbs take their goal in the Object Focus; ,
. ; others illustrate a goal as an Instrument (conveyed object) or as a Referent (partitive object).
. At best, in this present work, we can record the various classes of verbs and give a few rép- .
‘ . 5 resentative stems ‘or roots for each, in order to.hint at. the.idiom of Aklanon. ; .
. he ’ . 7
. Another problem‘is oge of transitivity as opposed to intransitivity. Although this matter
: does not seem to be involved with thesverb-root per se, ‘nevertheless it occurs in the dialect. .
oy . . For example, the verb uli'_can mean “return, go home" [intransitive use] or "return, give
roy, . . back" [transitive use}. Therefore it'is,a,.member of both Class Il and Class VI. The verb
fago! can mean “hide oneself" [intransitive] or “hide (something)" {transitive]; the verd adto
oy ("go, be on the way") is definitely intransitive, but with a causative prefix, as in paadto , it
mF ould take an object and be used transitively ("cause someone to go"). are .
In either case, due to our lack of research into the problems at hand, the reader is left .
. 7 on his own to identify stem classes. For the native speaker no real problem is posed since _?
he has the verb classes “built in" after years of triai~and-error use. For the foreigner, .
, ; this brief section is meant to make him aware of the fact ‘that not all forms represented in
, 2 the affix chart (Stetion 7) are applicable to every verb. A knowledge of two things, namely -
, sease and idiom wathin Aklanon,, becomes imperative. Sense alone can discover that verbs wo:
Loy ' of motion, for example, take no object, and thezefore they cannot be inflected with Object
' Focus forms. Idiom, on the other hand, is not readily, available, and will be a slow and par-
; . tial revelation through careful study., To Know that, bakae emphasizes an object with Odjezt :
Focus, winle baligya’ emphasizes an object with Instrument. Focus is a matter of unconscious
. but operable knowledge to the Aklanoy, who sees the grammatical distinction between "ouy" .
and "sell" which 1s in vhe roots of Aklanon idiom. But such knowledge is a matter of long and
—~ . ‘ careful research to the foreign-learner. | ’ :
oN.

lx ; ; C. THE STATIVE VERBS. ; _
‘ . . , ae l. THEORY. There isa class of words in \klanon that presents itself as particularily
47 oy x had to define or locate. The class has the following attributes or qualities: , .
. ot . 7 . (1) It is descriptive, or somewhat like the adjective class; how- ; .
te . . ever, it‘does not fit into that class because it does not have
. ‘ ' . standard inflection for degree. For example, the paradigm
. . . -. for adjectives in Aklanon reads like this: .
co putt! ("white")
; _ ; mas puti' ; (“whiter")
Lon ; ’ pinakamaputi’ . ("whitest") .
ov , but one cannot construct the paradigm: .
: . ; . *gutdm : ("hungry") : .
an ; °mas gutdm (“tiungrier") ,
a *pinakegutdm ("hungriest"): 4 ; ;
. aan ° . (2) ‘The class has verbal affixes in its conjugation, but the dif- .
; . + ™ ferent sets are universally defective in Actor Focus forms. .
. : All have na- forihs denoting imperfective aspect'value or . .
“1% . present time, ‘ut usually this form carries Actor Focus
. . . naga-oy akd ("I am-tired.") .
; nagutém aké - J am hungry.") .
: . naohaw aké ("I am thirsty.") on
; an . . . - npatawo imaw - ("He was born.") .
: ; - nahueog iméw ("He fell."")
. ~ ~(3) Even though the class has verbal conjugation, ‘its forms are ,
-, . very different in most cases from those of the regular verb .
. . conjugation:
. ° owa' ko pagbakea ("I did not buy...") .
; owa’ akd gigutmia ("I am not hungry.")
, No such form as "gi-~-a" exists in the regular Yerb conju- ot
. . gation.
. (4) These verbs exist in several classes which canbe distinguished ‘ ;
~ - 7 Loo from the classes set up for regular verbs, andalso"from each
: eee wives other. - -
. _eeeo . tan NN .
- a Because of these particular facts, we have decided to treat these words as a Separate part .
of speech, particularily since they stand apart from either verbs [regular] or adjectives...
‘ . . Since they can be inflected for tense or aspect, they are definitely verbs; since they describe
. states of being, they are stative, and somewhat related to adjectives. Thus they are called
_ ¢ “STATIVE VERBS". ; ~
. . a
. The particular subdivisions or classes are introduced in this section on the basis of dif-
. - ferences in meaning or function, and also their frequency in current use of the vernacular
y and their coajugation. Some sets are rather large, others are relatively small. This 1s a
' phenomenon of the dialect which 1s simply recorded, though it cannot be explained from the
data at hand.
