os 7. THE Jtag- STATIVE VERB QUALIFIER. Stative verbs generally do not he-re quality
of a distributive or causative nature. However, the jtag- prefix can be added to many verb :
; ° roots (regular or stative) to make a stative verb with the meaning "feel like” or “might want ;
to". The general idea of this prefix is to internalize one's feelings. For example, saot is
. . & root meaning "dance", which is a regular verb describing an external action; however, if ,
one says "natagsaot ako", he is stressing an internal desire, “I icel like dancing."

» 0 D. SUBSTANTIVES: NOUNS. AND-NOMINALS. ys \ -
- : . ;
: _ 1, THEORY. if verbs are the most important part of any utterance, nouns follow as -
. : . the second most important. Where a verb is 2 key to a sentence, nouns follow’as the bulk ;
. of the sentence, serving as a greater part of the door to the total meaning of the sentence.
. : _ If verbs relate the basic elements of a sentence, those basic elements that are reJated are-
mn nouns or substantives. ~ 1
a Like the verb, the noun is extremely elusive to define. We are all acquainted with the .
general definition of a noun as the name of a.person, place or thing. Although we can clear~
ly see the names of persons or places as noyns;-the classification of "thing" is far too vague.
We know thar "pencil" (lapis), "cow" (baka), “house" (bac4y) and so on are “things” because .
. . they are concrete objects, but more or less philosophical ruminations about masses such as
. “water™ (tubi’), "blood" (dugé'); about qualities such as YT s" (kapueahan), “strength”
(kabaskog), Shonor" (kadungeanan); about actions such as “thefg™ (panakaw), “arrival” |
. . (paga bét), “worship” (panimba); or about abstract concepts life “love” (higugma), "“thongtt"
(paino-ino) , f "respect" (tahod) elude us and confuse the basic definition of a noun as a ,
\ JH addition, the basic definition-is circular or redundant since nown and name are two
forms for the same word. As Roberts says, “Notional classification of nouns.is really a
- classification of the matters of the universe, and this is not a major responsibility of gr
mar.*28 4 ,
; 1,1. NATURAL NOUNS. Since definition itself is so difficuir, perhaps the casi way * .
, to discover just what constitutes a noun is to find words which any Aklanon wopté indisput- .
, ably repard as nouns. Such forms would be of benefit, of course, to begi S at the '
; grammar of the dialect, both foreigners and native children. ~ . 7 7
~ Looking to the vague notional. definition we could list names o sons, places and .
‘ things for a start

. * Any of these words from-these three lists would strike 2 native speaker as a noun or name ,
word. Each of them, as we shall see, is not merely an isolated part of speech, but can be--
. , considered to be a noun--along with many words that do Rot occur on this list--due'to its °
; function within a sentence. The debatable “natural definition" has only limited use. . .

a Note how the accent on the last syllable distinguishes the noun from the verb form. How- ‘
ever this criterion for judging nominal quality is limited to a very small set of words and
: does not help much in our search for: definition of the Aklanon noun. : .
a 1,3. FORMAL DEFINITION. A Certain series of morphological peculiarities can show a ,
: word to belong to the noun class. These particular forms fall under three headings. :
. , (1) PLURALIZATION WITH mga . "niga" [as it is spelled by Aklanons, actually pronoun-
ced /manga/] is a function word which pluralizes the article it follows, It does not, in ef-
- fect, pluralize the noun form. This can be proven by three observations. First of all, the
. mga marker can be fused with the xo/do and ku markers, forming rong/dong and Kong. ~In
these cases, the fusion is a topic-plural or associate plural marker. Secondly, the inarkers
for personal names have their own Plural form (si is pluralized sanday; ni, nanday, etc.). ‘
' The pluralization 1s fused with the marker, not with the name, Thirdly, the mga can not be
used on.nouns that are inherently plural. These observations”? should establish mga” as'a .
, function word pluralizing the marker it follows rather than the noun it precedes.
However, because it functions as a marker, the form that follows it is automatically il-
sy ” lustrated as a noun. In addition, even if mga pluralizes the function word, such plurality.
‘ can only be shown on nouns, and not with the other parts of speech. This is to say that only . .
nouns can be plural; verbs, adjectives and adverbs are not pluralized in Aklanon. Thus we :
. can say that the following are nouns:
mga isda! ("fishes") mga gahom ("powers")
mga babayi ("women") mga haco (“lizards")
. : . mga baraka ("stores") ~ inga namuk ("mosquitoes") -4
mga dagom ("needles") Inga tudio' 7ers; toes")
: ; The same would be true for any word following rong or dong, which also shows topic position
. and plurality. Hence, pluralization in-Aklanon, as in English, is d sign of a noun form, al-
~ thongh the means of marking the plural in cach language is quite different indeed. .

2. STANDARD NOUN-MARKING DERIVATIONAL AFFIXES. There is a fourth type of :
: : . form that.shows a word to belong generallj to a noun class, but the set is so large that it
iy . . demands special treatment in a section a]l its own. The purpose of this section, then, isto
; ‘ discuss.all of the standard affixes which generally occur with roots to make noun forms. In .
. L i the interest of both simplicity and completeness, several groups have been established ac- :
: cording to morphological similarities. That is, the groups share or center around-a com- .
ro, \ mon form, such as, basic root word, ka-type prefix, occupational nouns, and sa oh. ; _
2,1. NOTE ON STRESS WITHIN NOUN FORMS. Before going into detail, one generaliza- ‘S
. tion can be made about the whole set of nouns as opposed to other parts of speech, particu- >
larily verbs. When a noun form and a verb form are homophonic (have the same sounds), .
. * _ baere is usually a basic difference in stress. Verb affixes, which are inflectional, 30 tend to
‘ . carry the accent with them, or to influence the accent so that it falls at the end of the root -
: . word. Noun affixes, which are derivational, 30 tend to leave the accent within the root word, °
vo although normal accent changes may be necessitated (as in the case of 2, suffix at the end of _ .
. . the root word, which moves the accent towards the second to the last syllabte?4). For ex- :
ample, the root word sueéd ("enter") has the verb form sudlin ("be entered"). Note that
mo, the verb suffix -an carries the accent with it. Oa the other hand, the form kasudian ("“con* :
. tainer") is a noun. and the suffix -an in this case leaves the accent within the root word. .
7 \ -With the word hampang ("play") we have the verb iorm hampang4n, ‘ana the noun form ~ e
: hamp&ngan. With the root tapus ("end") we have the verb form manogtaptis ( just about .
. to end"), andthe noun form manostipus ("an ender, person who brings a thing to a close"). :
a The same is true of bunit ("angle"), with the verk form manogbunft (“may go angling") ‘and _
' : the noun form manogbinit ("an angler"). Tie noun prefix keeps the accent well within the om
' ’ root word, while the verb affix sends the accent to the last syllable. Lo wees . :
. Thus, noun forms generally retain the accent somewhere within the root word, while ;
. verb affixes strongly influence the placement of the accent or stress. ’

: 3. FUNCTION WORDS. Up until now we have been loo cing at two constituent elements
. or parts of speech, namely verbs and nouns. We have been speaking about them separately,
, outside of the sentences in which they occur, and seeing in what forms they can occur, This
. nas been a formal analysis in the sense that we paid attention to their form, more than to .
thelr.use. When one dea.s with nouns alone as isolated forms, such a procedure is possible;
but when one turns to the SUBSTANTIVE, which is a more complex nominal involving phrases
or clauses, it becomes necessary to discuss syntax (use) more than form. That is, we must
. sec how nouns and substantives fit into a sentence, not only how they iook in isolated units.
3,1. FUNCTION MARKERS. We have alxeady mentionec. that the function markers [ro/do,
: ku, it, sa) not only illustrate or point out a noun or substantive, but also define the element |
Or unit which follows them as a substantive. In other words, THE FUNCTION MARKERS |
DO NOT JUST POINT OUT SUBSTANTIVES OR NOMINALS; THEY MAKE SUBS'FANTIVES. |
Any word, phrase or clause which follows anu is connected to a function marker is made into |
: a nominal. Thus we do not merely have a matter of demonstration, but one of creation. |
’ , Since the function markers are not really content words and axe members of a cioscd . }
. : class, they do not properly belong in this section on "THE BASIC PARTS OF SPEECH." Sut,
a on the other hand, witaout 4 good knowledge of them and their use, one cannot understand
. the use of nouns ina sentence. Although they have little ox no lexical nieaning of their own,
: they serve to vary the function of the lexical words they gcvern. ,They are, as we have men~ |
: tioned {sce A, 3, this unit], the humble but indispensible servants of the substantive (nominal)
. . ccnstructions in Aklanon speech. Just four in number, they correspond to the four focus types .
discussed earlier [see B, 5, tins unit}. Focus allows an Aklanon to stress various situational
roles through the verb; markers or function words point out the various situational roles in
a the sentence as dictated by the verb, ana they illustrate both the function and the degree of
importance’ of the Substantives or nominals which thcy precede and govern.
. 3,2. OTHER TYPES OF FUNCTION WORDS. In Axlanon, as in most languages, we are
confronted with more than one type of norninal or subsvantive construction. The problem at
hand, of course, is to determine just how many types there are altogether. The solution-to
this problem involves at least two dimensions: . .
(1) First, we must find a Sentence structure that offexs what is called "a slot"; a cer~
tain function possibility which suits our purpose. We will then fill the slot with as many dif-
ferent types of phrases as we find in the dialect. Then we will sort them out and divide them
. into groups. Sach group that will be distinct forms a sud~class or sub-group of the various '
possible types of function words.
(2) Second, once we have deterniined how many types of function words there are that fit
into any one slot, we must then determine how many slots are available in the structure of .
‘ . Alanon speech. The results of this second search will bring us beyond the types of function °
words into the uses of function words.
Let us undertake together this two-fold search. =~
3,3. FINDING THE TYPES OF FUNCTION WORDS depeads on finding a sentence which can
‘ offer us a slot where all--or at least most-~of the typos could fit. Any sentence with.an ac-
tor that might apply to both persons or things would be ideal, since we need a cross~section
that could be represented hy almost any nominal, Such a sentence is not easy to find. Mf,
for example, we px < the sentence ".. -went to church yesterday," we can not filt the slot
with thnes hike "the rock" or "the tree", if we required common sense to be used along with

3,4. THE TOPIC SLOT.’ The chart given previously represents all the possible function
, words and/or substantives that can occurfin a single slot, namely the Topic slot [ox Actor
. slot in this particular case] in Aklanon speech, We can therefore subdivide Aklanon func-
- . tion words into the following groups:
(1) COMMON NOUNS, which are mazked by ro or do {which are allomorphs of each _
other and can generally be alternated, though ro is the most preferred fo 'm, except when .
following the sounds /d,t,n/, when do is preferred. Note that larger common nouns phra~ .
~ ses can be built and are included in this same slot. ,
(2) PERSONAL NAMES, the names of people or animals, which are marked by si [if
. and when a singular naine 1s given} or by sanday [if and when a name representing a plural
. eae . .
. group is given]. Aktanon, therefore, sets up a strict difference between common and per-
. . sonal nouns; we must say ro bayi, but si Maria; ro mga bayi, but sanday Maria. It
, shouid also be noted that personal names only apply to people. It does not include the broad
, class of proper names in English, For example, tic names of towns or countries ar» marked
: by the common noun markers, not by the personal name markers:
sa tawo (“to the person") ’
. kay Carlos ("to Carlos") .
; sa Kalibo ("to Kalibo") -
i” sa América ("to America") : "
/ (3) PRONOUNS, which are unmarked by any extra function word.;, bat have the function
-- built into them, The set given is the topic set {ako, ikaw, imaw, kami, kita, kamo, sanda}
. and serves m the Topic slot. In other slots, we will find that there axe different pronoun .
sets. This phenomenon is similar to English, which also has pronouns serving in various
. function positions ["I, you, he..." as opposed to "ine, you, him..."]. \y
° (4) DEICTICS [the place or demonstrative nominals}, which ore also unmarked by sep-
arate function words, but carry the function wittin them, They serve in defimte function
slots, and the set presented [raye, ruydn, ranha’, xatd} is the set for Topic position, The
Aklanon deictics, as we shall see, are set up accoxding to the same criteria as the pronoun
system, and therefore have four forms [based on "this, near the speaker (near me}; that,
near the listener [near you]" and so on}. The English equivalents "this" and "that" only have
two forms, based on entirely different criteria. .
7 . . :
(5) FUNCTION NOUNS, which are unmarked by any forinal marker [such as ro/do] if
when they serve in Topic position. However, as we shall discover later, when they
Serve in any or all other positions [assocrate, object, 1eferent}, they must be marked by
. the regular function markers [ku, it, sa]. Thus, they only need to be distinguished as
o “separate class when in Topic position; in all other slots, they serve as ana function as .
: * common nouns. .

\ tion; if the Associate slot comes after the verb, then the verb is part of a verb phrase. Thus,
\ the prepositive Associate is a NOUN DETERMINER in that it transforms the part of speech °
following it into a noun or nominal construction, whereas the postpositive Associate is a
MODIFIER in that It only modifies or describes the word or phrase it follows.

\ . . 4,4. THE PRONOUN SYSTEM. After having discussed the larger class of substantive phra-
ses, we come to another class of substantives: the pronoun. Just seven words, their impor-
‘ . tance far outweighs theix number. They are often classified as a separate part of speech,
. but their relationship to the markers and to the function slots of nouns makes it far more
. convenient and reliable to treat them as a subgroup of nouns or substantives.
. Modern grammar shies away from defining pronouns since every definition meets some
sort of philosophical failure. As their name implies, they "stand for nouns" or take the _
. place of nouns; hence, they are true substantives. Yet phrases and clauses also stand for .
nouns, and yet are not pronouns, The only way we can arrive at a somewhat Suitable defi-
; nition is to say what they are, and what they can do Without:
. ~ (a) Pronouns are substantives that never have a marker in front _
: of them; they stand alone, without the ro/do or other markers. ,
; : (b) They never have @ pluralizing marker (like mga or sanday), a .
. . _ ‘ but rather have differing forms for singular and plural.
: ‘ (c) They have a special form for each function, which does not
. ; , involve the standard function markers [as mentioned in (a)].
In other words, we find them corresponding to al} substantives in that they have plurals and -
. operate within each function (though like the personal names, they do not have 2 direct ob-
ject or goal form); yet they du 2° tis a way unique to themselves. Two more Points can be
made: .
(d) The pronouns correspond very closely to the proper name ;
“ot markers [particularily-ay, ni and kay] in that they carry -
certain morphophonemic: similarities, and also do not have
a function as direct object or goal. °
(e) The Aklanon pronouns, 2s ‘opposed to those of English, are
: totally speaker-listener oriented. They are set up accord=
ing to the following format:

5. SUBSTANTIVES USED IN NON-FOCUS CONSTRUCTIONS. Until now we have been .
discussing the relaticustup of the function markers to verb centers. We have seen how they
; SO, are directly linked to focus forms in the verb. It is also possible that the function words be .
. : . used independently of fecus, usually to link a nominal.or substantive construction to another
substantive or noun center. In this instance, the markers or function words do not mark
case or function, but rather show attributes of that particular noun center.
. 5,1. ATTRIBUTIVE USES of substantives link a noun-center to another noun-center with .
« various effects, depending on the function Class used. .
(1) ATTRIBUTIVE USE OF THE TOPIC. Examples of the topic markers occuring at~ 7
tributive to a noun-center are as follows: . \ .
_) "Imdw nagakinahangean it kamisadentro nga put!" nga matag-od

ae nga puti' nga matag-od go butkon."” The nga linker would not be appropriate in place of do
pecause it would mane Buthon ("shoulder; sleeve") equivalent to the adjective puti’ (white)
z . and matag-od ("short"). Such a construction would be confusing ¢ince butkon ts not an adj-
. . ective in tus case, but part of an adjective phrase "matag-od do butkon", which in qurn is .
part of the even larger adjective clause as stated above. Thus, to be cleax and to show the
- amplification of the adjective matag-od, the marker do is used as an attributive. ; \

. shows the expanded or amplified Subject more Clearly. > .

RB. ADJECTIVES. - |
. \ |
1. THEORY. The desire for precision in modern linguistics makes it difficult, if not |
. impossible to define any part of speech. For any definition suggested, there are usually |
; - a host of exceptions. If we say, for example, that nouns name substances and that adjec= ;
. v tives name qualities, the philosopher would ask us to prove that cither of those elusive terms
: aciucily exist, and we would find the idea of either of them melting in our minds. |
1,1. NATURAL AD'ECTIVES. Looking for words which name qualities, we can find the , ;
. following forms in Aklanon that seem to be natural adjectives: 3 7
itém ("black") cunét ("rotten") Co
puti* ("white") hilaw ("xaw, unripe") |
: pued ("xed") hiléng ("drunk") |
‘ . bata’ (“young”) kambang _ (""two~colored!) .
; . eagi, (“old, ancient") Is6g (“brave, daring") \ |
coh Certain words;-then;-automatically fit the definition of words which name qualities, but |
. ° they do not account for the entire class of adjectives. .
‘s 7 1,2. SYNTACTIC DEFINITION. It is a fact that nouns can be said to name, but adjec-
: . tives do not in effect name, they describe, modify, reinforce or otherwise limit. Hence, ,
.. ‘ ; we fird not a notional definition, but rather a syntactic definition which says a ithe bit
. more about adjectives. . .
~ the primary identifiable quality about adjectives {s their ability to fill the slote left
. ' , blank in a structure such as: .
: . 7 “ro tawo nga...(1)...hay medyo nga...(2)..." i .
, ("The ...man is somewnat...") m
. These two positions may be described as (1) following the nga linker and describing 2 nomi~ ;
. nal form 1n 2 substantive clause pound tugether by a function marker; (2) following the liga-
3 ture nga in 4 descriptive clause marked by a qualifier (such as medyo) . This frame is in- ‘
. tended to be a type of criterion to define adjectives, and is of grammatical interest only, Ob-
viously, most of the statements dexived will be cixculax, yet they will reveat ay adjective
, form in a paradigm: ;
(a2) "Ro tawo nga mapueang hay medyo nga mabuét." .

If we adopt this frame, we must accept the consequence that not every adjective form . 0
. will fit. On the other hand, there may be some other parts of speech which would fit by
Sense, but yet not be true adjectives. If we examine the problem more closely, we will
find certain criteria with regard to the form of adjectives that settle the problem more sa-
tisfactorily. The formal definition of adjectives will take us into the next two sections. ‘The
first will deal with the basic affixes or derivational affixes most common to adjective forms.
The second will deal with the comparison or degree of adjectives. :
. 1,3. GENERAL DEFINITION OF ADJECTIVES. In light of the previous observations, we
. - ean Say the following about adjectives: ;
. (1) They are predicat've or descriptive words. .
(2) They have no infl, on for focus, aspect or tense lke verbs; but they do have
inflection for degree, am ‘e compaxed or used in comparisons.
. (3) Whereas verbs hav pussible form in ha~ , most adjectives have a root form
. in ma~, and they have no fo. 1 in na-. °
. (4) Adjectives can occur aftc- qualifiers which Thdicate the degree to which an adjective
. 1S meant to apply. These qualifiers illustrate an adjective to be an adjective; they point out
adjective constructions.
. 2. STANDARD ADJECTIVE-MAKING DERIVATIONAL AFFIXES. There are a number of
affixes which commonly occur with adjective bases or other bases to make up adjective forms. .

ucularily between the ma- verb prefix and the ma-~ adjective prefix. Generally, there is no
_ . difference in stress or accent, and the form must be known through its meaning ahd/or con-
_ text in the clause or sentence. For the sake of simplicity and completeness, the various
° forms have been grouped together, ceutering around a common fornt. .

, 3. THE DEGREE OF ADJECTIVES. Both adjectives and verbs are predicatives. They ;
. predicate or say something about nouns or nominals. Verbs predicate, as we have seen, by
means of tense, aspect, mode, focus and quality. Adjectives predicate, or say what they
. . have to sav, by degree. They tell just how far the quality they describ applies.
In Aklanon, the ma- adjective class illustrates, as well as simple adjective bases or ,
roots, some six distinct levels or. degrees:
" 1. POSITIVE, which simply qualifies or describes”
‘ 2. DIMINUATIVE, which weakens the quality of the description;
3. RELATIVE, whici predicates a mildly unequal relationship;
4, INTENSIVE, whic4 illustrates a great degree of a quality;
5. SUPERLATIVF, w uch illustrates the highest degree of a quality. ,
We will discuss these various degrees u.ividually . . “

: ; F. DISCOURSE PARTICLES. ,
. ; Discourse particles, including the enclitic adverbs (so-called because of the position they
> 7 occupy is. their clauses), are crucial to meaningful and idiomatic Aklanon speech, For the :
eo . _ foreign learner, they present quite a problem. They are often extremely. difficult to trans .
i. late into English or to handle in Aklanon, because they are idiomatic to the dialect, and are
Poe \ not found in English. ' In most cases they take months of practice to handle properly. .
4 ’ Markers, we observed, precede the words or phrases they go with, and they give func-
v _ tion. Discourse particles,, on the other hand, generaily follow the words or phrases they go ‘
: ; - with, and they give color~-without at all interfering with function. To the extent that they
7 add meaning or color; they are somewhat adverbial. Those that follow the word or phrase .
= they color are called enclitics or postpositive particles. However, not all of the discourse
: . particles follow the word or phrase they influence; some go before the word, and some come. :
y . at the very end of the clause. What is common to them all-is that they.are particles andnot - :
: | . really distinct parts of speech (in the same way, for example, that a noun or adverb is). They x
“ 4 . also color the speech of a person, however each particle with its own-shade of meaning.
to, _ 1, THE ENCLITIC OR POSTPOSITIVE DISCOURSE PARTICLES. The largést class of
: a eiscour-e particles is the enclitics., Their distribution in terms of function and meaning . .
koa ~ ig quite varied, but all of them follow the same type of word order~-they must follow the 7
pot _ word or phrase they. color immediately. In our following discussion, many of the ‘transla~ :
x . . tions are idiomatic to the situation in English, rather than literal or even.accurate transla~ ;
: “ tions of the enclitics.. ‘
oS . ; 1,1. THE QUESTION AND ANSWER PARTICLES are as follows: \ . ,.
an , ; ; , . baes {question particle meaning "indeed?, .really?"} , Co
. -a ‘(question suffix, meaning "might itbe?"} . : ;

my . ; : or reason) : . . .

oO . Aklanon does not ordinarily use a particle to identify every question. However, a speaker .

at, (a) "Sify bacd imaw?" «| ("Where in the world is he?") .
a ; , i. (b) "Ik4w baed ro among maestra?" (“Are you really going to be our teacher?") :

me ’  Conjunctives are linkers of a sort, but instead of-linking st:nple words together in modic 3
n -* , * slex fashion (such as nouns, and adjective, verbs and adverbs), they link’together elements | ;
wl, " 4 ; (words, phrases or clauses) in a way and with a meaning‘all their own. There are two types :
iL y re of conjunctives: coordinating and subordinating. ne
a 1, COORDINATING CONJUNCTIONS are-normally used.to connect sentence elements of — I, .
*. Mn . the same grammatical class; for example, nouns with nouns, adverbs “ith adverbs, clauses . :

ee . elements they join together. Subordinating conjunctions, we shall see, must always stand” ns
3 ‘, ., * . at the beginning of the element with which they occur; and discourse particles staid'either = -

‘ ; immedigtely before or immediately after the element with which they occur. Examples of :
